D Copies of requested military D personnel D medical 
records are attached. We suggest you make an extra copy and guard against losslor damage. 
We regret if any photocopies may be of poor quality, but they are the best copies obtainable. 
D The attached separation document may include the following information: authority for separation, reason for separation, Reenlistment Eligibility Code, 
and Separation (SPN/SPD) Code. If you require a copy of the separation document that does not contain the above information, you may request a deleted 
copy from this Center. A seal has been affixed to the enclosed copy to attest to its authenticity. 
D Title 32, Part 45 of the Code of Federal Regulations prohibits this Center from providing to third parties copies of separation docume_nts which contain 
the type and character of discharge, authority and narrative reason for separation, reenlistment eligibility code, and separation program designator/number. 
Ifyou require a copy ofthe separation document containing the above information, the veteran must specifically authorize the release to you of an UNDELETED 
copy of the separation document. 
D The Privacy Act of 1974 does not permit the release of a social security number or other personal information to the public without the authorization of 
the veteran concerned; therefore, we have deleted personal identifying data relating to other persons. 
D Since September 1979 the military service departments have not issued individual separation documents for each enlistment period if the person was 
discharged for immediate reenlistment. Block 18 of the DD Form 214 issued at final separation reflects the full period of continuous active military service. 
0 The Reenlistment Eligibility (RE) Code issued upon releaS{l from active duty on __________ is ____________ 
D The reason and authority for separation from active duty/discharge on ______is,____________________ 
D The record of service in the _________ indicates being in a POW status from _________ to _________ 
0 A discharge certificate is issued only as an original document; thus, there is no file copy from which to make a copy for you. We will obtain an appropriate 
substiMe certificate for a veteran or surviving spouse if you will tell us whether the original was: 1) never received; or (2) lost or destroyed. 
D The document you have requested, DD Form 214, Report of Separation, was not used until Jan. 1, 1950. However, a similar form was used at the time 
the person named above was separated. A copy of it is attached. A seal has been affixed to the enclosed copy to attest to its authenticity. 
D When the person named above was separated, it was not the practice to issue a document which served as a report of separation. 
D The original report of separation was issued at the time of separation. Another original cannot be issued. The attached copy, however, will serve the same 
purpose as the original. 
D No report of separation was issued, since the person named above had no active service, or less than 90 days of active duty for training. 
D The service record of the person named above does not contain a copy of a report of separation, or its equivalent. Therefore, we are instead providing 
the attached NA Form 13038, Certification of Military Service. This will serve as verification of military service and may be used for any official purpose. 
0 That portion of your request seeking medals/awards has been referred to the office checked below, which has jurisdiction over the issuance of 
medals/awards. Any further correspondence on this subject should be addressed to that office. Both offices are at 9700 Page Ave., St. Louis, MO 63132. 
0 ARPERCEN, Attn: DARP-VSE-A 
O Navy Liaison Office, Room 5409 
D That portion of your request regarding Air Force or Army Air Corps medals will be answered under separate cover. 
D The medical records reques1ed 
D The documents requested pertaining to discharge 
have been lent to the Department of Veterans Affairs (VA). 
Please phone the VA at 1-800-827-1000 for help in obtaining these records, and provide your VA Claim Number, 
. 
D The Department of Defense Privacy Program, 32 CFR 310.30(f), allows for the disclosure of medical records to the individual to whom they pertain. A 
portion ofthe requested medical records, however, contains information which can be interpreted and explained properly only by a physician. If you wish us 
to send copies to a designated physician, please provide us with the name and address of that physician. The request MUST INCLUDE the written consent

request, this form, and any other form you complete. 
0 Copies of requested military D personnel D medical 
records are attached. We suggest you make an extra copy and guard against loss or damage. 
We regret if any photocopies may be of poor quality, but they are the best copies obtainable. 
0 The attached separation document may include the following information: authority for separation, reason for separation, Reenlistmant Eligibility Code, 
and Separation (SPN/SPD) Code. If you require a copy of the separation document that does not contain the above information, you may request a deleted 
copy from this Center. A seal has been affixed to the enclosed copy to attest to its authenticity. 
0 Title 32, Part 45 of the Code of F_ede1 al Regulations prohibits this Center from providing to third parties copies of separation documents which contain 
the type and character of discharge, authority and narrative reason for separation, reenlistment eligibility code, and separation program designator/number. 
If you require a copy of the separation document containing the above information, the veteran must specifically authorize the release to you of an UNDELETED 
copy of the separation document. 
0 The Privacy Act of 1974 does not permit the release of a social security number or other personal information to the public without the authorization of 
the veteran concerned; therefore, we have deleted personal identifying data relating to other persons. 
0 Since September 1979 the military service departments have not issued individual separation documents for each enlistment period if the person was 
discharged for immediate reenlistment. Block 18 of the DD Form 214 issued at final separation reflects the full period of continuous active military service.

substitute certificate for a veteran or surviving spouse if you will tell us whether the original was: 1) never received; or (2) lost or destroyed. 
0 The document you have requested, DD Form 214, Report of Separation, was not used until Jan. 1, 1950. However, a similar form was used at the time 
the person named above was separated. A copy of it is attached. A seal has been affixed to the enclosed copy to attest to its authenticity. 
D When the person named above was separated, it was not the practice to issue a document which served as a report of separation. 
0 The original report of separation was issued at the time of separation. Another original cannot be issued. The attached copy, however, will serve the same 
purpose as the original. 
0 No report of separation was issued, since the person named above had no active service, or less than 90 days of active duty for training. 
D The service record of the person named above does not contain a copy of a report of separation, or its equivalent. Therefore, we are instead providing

CONDITIONS APPLICABLE TO RECIPIENTS OF CARDS 
Recipients of the Uniformed Services Identification and Privilege Card (s) authorized on the basis of this application w ill be 
responsible for proper use of the privileges and facilities authorized. 
Recipients will surrender cards immediately upon call to do so 
o r when appropriate under applicable regulations, and will notify the agencies designated to grant authorizations for privileges and 
facilities in event of any change in status affecting eligibility therefor. 
Medical care furnished in uniformed services facilities is sub­
ject to the availability of space, facilities, and the capabilities of the medical staff to provide such care. 
Determinations made by the 
medical officer o r contract surgeon, o r his designee, as to the availability of space, facilities, and the capabilities of the medical staff 
shall be conclusive. 
Reimbursement shall be required for any unauthorized medical care furnished at Government expense.

MEMORANDUM FOR SECRETARY OF THE AIR FORCE PERSONNEL COUNCIL

SUBJECT - Colonel Howard M. McCoy, S50A, USAF

1. Attached is application for retirement submitted by Colonel
Howard M. McCoy for consideration and decision.

2. Colonel McCoy has completed more than 24 years active Federal
service and is eligible for consideration for retirement in accordance
with Section 5 of the Act of Congress approved 31 July 1935, as amended
(Paragraph 6a, AFR 36-50).

3. Colonel McCoy's request for retirement is based on the fact
that he has been assigned for the past few years in a specialized area
in the development of physical security and allied equipment and that
his military potential in any other field is limited, He further states
that in view of the termination of this agency by the Defense Department
there is an even greater need for its continuation as a commercial
activity.

4, While he is recognized as a specialist in this former field
his overall military record indicates he possesses sufficient potential
to be of continued value to the Air Force, Therefore, it is recommended
that his application for voluntary retirement be not favorably considered

at this time,

HOWARD MONROE MOCOY

SUBJECT: 
Retirement - Colonel Howard M. RlcCoy, 550A, USAF 
1. 
Attached is application for retirement submitted by 
Colonel Howard M. McCoy for consideration and decision. 
2. 
Colonel McCoy has completed more than 24 years active 
Federal service and is eligible for consideration for r etirement 
in accordance with Section 5 of the Act of Congr ess approved 31 
July 1935, as amended (Paragraph 6a , AFR 36-50). 
3. This officer has been assigned for a number of years 
in a specialized area in the development of physical security, 
and allied equipment and it is believed his military potential 
in any other field is limited. 
4. 
A review of his service indicates that he has reached or 
passed the peak of his effectiveness and he l acks motivation for 
an assi gnment in a new field. 
Continued service will probably 
result in lower efficiency and culminate in a probl em of assign­
ment. 
5. 
With the possibility of increased forced attrition over 
the next few years, it is considered to be in the best interest 
of the Air Force to approve his request for retirement at this 
time.

ATTACHMENT

SUMMARY:

This attachment outlines the exceptional circumstances and factors
which dictate this officer's action in requesting voluntary Rete enna!
It reviews his specialized experience in the development of physical
security and allied equipment, which work has occupied most of his
time since 1946 and has made him the governmental authority in this
field,

It further describes the results of an informal unofficial survey
he has just completed which accents the widely accepted need for ime
provement in our national industrial security program, In view of
the recent abolishment of the Physical Security Equipment Agency of
which he was the Director, the most expedient way to fill the need
appears to be through the establishment of an independent civil organi.
zation working closely with the Office of Defense Mobilization and the
Department of Defense, If this request is approved he intends to

devote all of his efforts thereafter to this important task,

—_——.

Ref, Para, 2,f, (PART Il) Dept, Air Force Message, No, AFPMP.ABC
ALMAJCOM 660/54 dtd 11 June 1954

·• 
.., 
BACKGROUND: 
The decision to take this voluntary retirement action was taken 
only after the most thorough deliberation. 
It has been the most im.. 
portant one made by him in his entire military career, and it is also 
the most considered one, £or he has been preoccupied with it ever 
since 4 March 1954 when a De part:Inent of D efense Directive abolished 
the Physical Security Equipment Agency (PSEA) of which he was the 
Director. H e had been sel ected to head that Agency at its inception 
and served £or the three ye a rs of its existenc e . 
During that time 
the Agency became the r e cognized authority within the govermnent in 
the field of security equipment and it has an excellent record of un­
selfish service to t he many agencies with which it was associated. 
He had also been serving as Chairman of the F ederal Committee on 
Security Equipment since its organization in the £all of 1953. 
Since 1946 this officer has devoted practically all of his efforts 
(about 20, 000 hours) fighting C ommunism through technical means 
by d eveloping security and investigative e quipment. He conscious ly 
selected this specialized fie l d of work in lieu of a conventional car eer 
in aeronautical R e s earch arid Development with full appreciation of 
the lack of opportunity fo r advancement in the more specialized field. 
H e was firmly convinced that he could best serve the interests of 
2 
.
•

national security by such action. He realized that: -
Just as a nationls 
organization for war depends on the technical weapons which are on hand .. 
so does a nation1s facilities security program depend on the technical 
security equipments which are available! It is estimated that less than 
10% of the technical equipment considered necessary to implement a 
realistic facilities security program has been developed to date. We are 
in an advanced technological age yet our guard systems, both industrial 
and military, have changed little since biblical days! 
As Deputy Commanding General (T-2 Intelligence), then Chief, 
Intelligence Department. Headquarters, Air Materiel Command, for 
the period December 1946 ... August 1949, the undersigned managed a 
specialized laboratory kno.wn as the Science Survey Office {SSO) which 
developed certain classified types of security and investigative equip. 
ment. That laboratory was abolished for unknown reasons shortly after 
subject officer was transferred August 1949 to the Industrial College of 
the Armed Forces. From there he was assigned to the Central Intelligence 
Agency, leaving 1 March 1951 to become Director of the newly activated 
Physical Security Equipment Agency. Copies of pertinent directives are 
attached as Tabs A, B, and c. The need for such an activity as the 
SSO had continued to exist after its close ...out in 1949. The advent of the 
Korean War so accentuated this need that the Management Committee, 
Office of the S ecretary of Defense, after detailed deliberations, established 
3 
I

i
PSEA to ensure extension of the work of the earlier laboratory (SSO). 
PSEA was a joint project under the management control of the Depart.. 
ment of the Air Force with the r e sponsibility for providing for the 
development of physical security and related investigative equipment 
as a common service for all agencies of the Department of Defense. 
It is estir:nated that PSEA had met only about 10% of its chartered 
responsibilities during its existence. There are several reasons for 
this state of performance ; they include: 
a. The initial small staff of 25 was never increased, as 
was planned, in proportion to the work..load that developed. 
b. R&D funds for programmed activities were frozen as 
of March 1953 after less than two years of operation, the first 
funds not being m ade available until M ay 1951. 
c. The Agency never operated under its revised Charter 
(Tab C) which would have insured full cooperation of the 
Military Services through the PSEA Policy Board. 
The Agency's efforts concerning security equipment were just 
coming to fruition in the shape of a d etailed R&D program, as the 
result of a lmos t three years of background research work, when its 
untimely end came. Government agencies other than the Department 
of Defense which had come to depend a lmost sol ely on PSEA on 
physical security matters dealing with improved equipment included 
the GSA, State Department, AEC, NSA and CIA. 
4 
,

The matter of the Agency's continued existence had been under con.. 
sideration by the Deputy Secretary of Defense since March 1953. 
The 
three Military Departments and the Office, Domestic Security Programs 
of OSD, all represented on the PSEA Policy Board, are on record as 
having supported the continuance of the Agency1s activities, thus verifying 
the need for its services particularly in the security equipment field 
which constituted about 85% of its effort the last year of operations. 
The Agency and its functions were abolished 4 March 1954 per 
D epartment of Defense Directive No. 5 154. 9 (Tab D). Although there 
was no stated reason, it is surmised that this action was intended in the 
interests of economy for it is understood that some other activities were 
stricken from the FY 1955 budget following the informal principle that 
any R&:D activity would be dropped that did n ot directly sup port a weapon 
or weapons system already in or scheduled for production. This con... 
tradiction between the existing need for the Agency's services and the 
act of abolishing the Agency and its functions, leads to the l onclusion 
that the Department of Defense does not conside r the development of 
security equipment to be its responsibility. This apparently leaves the 
matter up to industry. 
This preznise appears to be consistent with 
administrative policy. 
THE NEED: 
Assuming that the present policy of the Department of Defense in 
5

- . ‘ «Be
. ‘ “54 2 =
v ' ' '
Se
: f

regards to its industrial security program is limited to the statement of
policies and principles, it is apparent that the most practical and sensible
way to fill the void between policy and an adequate working security
system in the defense industries is to follow the precedent of the Civil
Defense and disaster control organizations and have the Government ene
courage the formation of an independent civil project in accordance with
established principles of our competitive freeeenterprise economy,Such
a project would eventually be a large one and should preferably be a none
profit organization of the highest standards in the tradition of Air Force
Project RAND, the Battelle Memorial Institute and others,

It is believed that the importance of protecting our defense industry
facilities (as well as our military installations) against sabotage does
not require documentation here as it has been a subject of comment by
the President of the United States, members of Congress, and other
Government officials for some time, The Director of the Federal
Bureau of Investigation has stated to a Congressional Committee to the
effect that the technical problems of his agency have increased ten-fold
in the past one and one-half decades, In connection with Mr, Hoover's
statement, there is substantial evidence that the USSR has spent an
estimated 50 times more effort in the past 20 years than has been ex.
pended in this country on equipment matters associated with those of

PSEA, Itis noted that existing burglary protection systems of the

& = * i)

central station type are less than fifty percent effective in preventing
penetration of the protected areas by unskilled burglars, On the other
hand, protection against thoroughly trained saboteurs must be near
perfect because the saboteur can be considered to have accomplished
his mission once the penetration has been effected,

It is estimated that overall physical security in our Key defense
industries can be improved in the order of four to five hundred per.
cent over a period of several years by the use of proper security
equipment and systems, The sums now being spent by the Departe
ment of Defense for industrial and military security are described by
the Industrial Security Division (OSD) as "staggering'"’ and are exe
clusive of the security clearance program, The amount is classified
so it is not included here, A gain of several hundred percent in the
overall physical security of our Strategic Air Command bases should
be possible and would be of inestimable value, When it is considered
that it costs an average of $20,000.00 per year to man one guard post
in industry using civilian guards, and that military guards cost about
$40,000, 00 per year per post, it appears possible to replace much of
this fallible human factor with advanced technological equipment with
a resulting gainin security, plus substantial savings in both money

1/

and manpower,

CELE Mie ene ree i ee Nr LR
l/ Article dated 15 March 1954, "Security Guard Costs", Office of
fhe Assistant SecDef, Research & Development, Washington 25, D.C.

THE REMEDY:

During periods of leave since 4 March 1954, while acting unofficially
and solely as an individual, this officer has consulted informally with
representative industrialists and with casualty insurance and plant PrOe
tection officials concerning industrial security problems and what an
individual such as himself might be able to accomplish were he retired
from active service, He has also talked with pertinent personnel within
the Department of Defense and Government and from this research has
established that there is a nationawide need within our Defense industries
for the formation of an Industrial Security Engineering Service (ISES),
or the like, to serve the same purpose regarding protection against
sabotage as the various commercial Fire Protection services accome
plish in regard to protection against fire, It has also been established
that no such service as an ISES now exists, The reaction to his proe
posed solution to the problem has been most encouraging and has been
accepted informally as being soundly conceived in principle, operationally
practicable and economically justifiable; If properly implemented, it
has promise to fill a serious void in our industrial security program
which otherwise cannot be expected to be supplied for many years now
that the PSEA organization and functions have been abolished,

Subject officer is tentatively scheduled to be assigned in the near

future to Headquarters, Air Materiel Command, where presumably he
will engage in procurement work, His primary AFSC is Research and
Development Director (8416) with secondary AFSC - Staff Intelligence
Officer (2016). Including postgraduate schooling, he has served in
these categories for the past twenty years, Because of his experience,
subject officer considers himself to be the logical individual to under-
take such a civil project as outlined above and is willing to sacrifice
the almost certain security that continued military service would afford
in order to make the attempt.

He has the deepest and most sincere convictions that the best ine
terests of national security will be served if he is permitted to retire
from active military service to dedicate himself to servea cause for

which he is most uniquely qualified,

Respectfully submitted,

Mrnvardf MW Me
HOWARD M, McCOY
Colonel, USAF 550A

THE SECRETARY OF DEFENSE

WASHINGTON

CHARTER

Physical Security Equipment Agency

Pursuant to the authority vested in the Secretary of Defense by the
National Security Act of 1947, as amended, there is hereby established, as
a joint project under the manazement control of the Department of the Air
Force, a Physical Security Equipment Asency (hereinafter called the "Agency")
with the responsibility for providing for the development of physical
security and related investigative equipment as a common service for all
agencies of the Department of Defense.

I, DEFIUITIONS

1. Physical security equipment is that used to provide warning of
and protection against covert and other clandestine activities involving:

a, Military installations and their sensitive areas,
vb. Materiel,

c. Classified documents,

d. Personnel,

This definition is not intended to preclude the Agency calling to the
attention of elements of the Department of Defense applications of such
equipment to other than covert or clandestine uses or to deny the services
of the Agency to such elements in developing such equipment on request,

&, Investigative equipment is that required by the pertinent Depart-
ment of Defense agencies for:

&, Oriminal and related type investigative activities,
b. Counter-intelligence and counter-sabotage activities.
Cc. Intelligence activities,
II, DUTIES
Subject to the authority of the Secretary of Defense and in consonance

with the requirements of the various agencies of the Department of Defense,
the Agency shell perform the dutiss listed below:

Ls For the solution of physical security and investigative problens,
review pertinent basic and applied research and development projects and
equipment, both domestic and “orcisn, and evaluate the feasibility of

Department of Defense Directive
• 
SUBJECT 
Physical Security Equipment Agency 
Pursuant to the authority vested in me by the National. Security 
Act 0£ 1947, as amended, there is hereby reconstituted in the Depart­
ment of Defense, as a joint agency under the management of the 
Department of the Air Force, the Physical Security Equipment Agency 
(hereina.fter called the "Agency"). It shall be the principal. Agency 
within its field in the Department of Defense for providing for the 
development of physical. security and related investigative equipment 
as a common service for all agencies of thP Department of Defense, 
and shall be responsible .for effecting coordination with all similar 
activities in the Department of Defense. 
I DEFINITIONS 
1. 
Physical security equipment is that used to provide waming 
of and protection against covert and other clandestine activities 
involving: 
a. Militar-f installations and their sensitive areas. 
b. 
Materiel. 
c. 
Classified documents. 
d. Personnel, 
This definition is no~ intended to preclude the Agency from 
calling to the attention of elements of the Department of Defense 
applications of such equipment to other than covert or clandestine 
uses or to deny the services of the Agency to such elements in 
developing such- equipment on request. 
2. 
Investigative eq'.lipment is tha.t required by the pertinent 
Department of Defense agencies for: 
a. Criminal and related type investigative activities. 
b. 
Counte!'-intelligence and counter-sabotage activities. 
c. Intelligence activities.

• 
II 
ORGANIZATION 
1. 
There is established a Policy Board consisting of the 
Assistant Chief of Staff, G-2, Army; the Director of Naval Intelli­
gence; the Director of Special Investigations, Air Force, and a 
representative appointed by the Secretary of Defense, nth the 
responsibilities hereinafter outlined. 
The chairmanship of the 
Policy Board shall be rotated yearly between Board members represent­
ing the Military Departments. 
The Air Force member of the Board 
shall serve as the initial Chairman. 
2. 
The Agency shall be composed of a Director, a .Deputy 
Director and an Agency staff . 
III DUTIES AND RESPONSIBILITIES 
Subject to the author i ty and direction of the Secretary of 
Defense and in consonance 1fith t he- requirements of the various agencies 
of the Department of Defense:

operations; provide programs and make such -review of 
the operational procedures of the Agency as may be 
necessary to insure proper consideration of security, 
a

responsible for assuring t hat t heir agency's require­
ments are made known to t he Agency, and that equipment 
developed by the Agency is made available for use 
Within their agencies. 
c. 
Approve all projects of the Agency and estaulish 
priorities of such projects. 
d. 
Provide the Agency wi.th selected i ntelligence per­
taining to foreign devel opments and applications of 
security and intelligence equipment.

a.

t he equipment, or adapting the already demonstrated 
phenomena and principles.,

Secretary of Defense and submit such interim program 
reports as may be advisable. All such reports are to 
be sutmitted to the Secretary of Defense through the 
Policy Board and the Executive Agency. 
IV .ADMINISTRATION 
1. 
The Secretary of the Air Force is designated as "executive 
agent" for t he Agency. 
As such, he shall be responsible to the 
Secretary of Defense for the operations of the Agency, and shall,

19 Jan 53 
R-51 
. 1 
2. Services ot the Agency shall be available to all Department 
of Defense agencies under procedures established by the Policy Board. 
3. In order to further the technical aspects or the Agency's 
programs, each military department shall designate a liaison ot.f'icer 
\ 
representing its principal. research and development stat£. F.ach 
; 
liaison officer, within hi s department, shall be responsible fort 
a. Arranging for the exchange of infomation concerning 
research and development programs being carried out i .n 
their Departments which may be pertinent to approved 
programs of the Agency. 
b. Arranging through authorized channels and lfithin personnel 
and budgetary limits for the conduct of research and 
development programs in their Departments, either by 
contract or utilization of Govennent facilities. upon 
request of the Agency. 
c. Providing within personnel and budgetary limits for a 
continuing program to insure the consi deration oi' the 
results of scientific research and development efforts 
in their departments for possible application in 
developing security and intelligence equipment. 
4. F.ach Service shall designate properly qualified personnel 
at appropriate echelons for liaison purposes consistent with detennina­
tions made in accordance with paragraph 1 of this Section. 
5. Exchange of infonnation by and with the Agency shall be 
governed by normal security practices.

Department of Defense Directive 
SUBJECT 
Physical Security Equipment Agency - Abolishment of 
1. The Physical Security F.quipment Agency, established by 
Department of Defense Directive No. R-5154.1, dated 19 January 
1953, is hereby abolished. 
2. 
The Secretary of the Air Force will take all action 
necessary to the orderly termination of the affairs of the Agency, 
to include: 
a. Disposition of all records, equipment, supplies, 
and facilities in accordance with applicable policy and 
procedures. 
b. Disposition of civilian personnel in accordance 
with applicable personnel policy and procedures. 
c. Disposition of military personnel assigned to 
the Agency in accordance with applicable reassignment 
procedures. 
J. The Secretary of the Air Fbrce may make such delegation 
of authority as he deems necessary for accomplishment of the above 
actions. 
4. Directive No. 5154. 9 (formerly No. R-5154.l), 19 January 
1953, is hereby cancelled.

Attention: Assistant Chief of Air Staff, Personnel.

1. Reference is made to letter, your headquarters, dated
28 February 19/44, which transmits letter of recommendation for
the promotion of Colonel Howard Monroe McCoy, 018054, Air Corps,
to the grade of colonel, Army of the United States.

ably considered the recommendation for the promotion of Colonel
McCoy. The promotion of an officer over the heads of so many
of his seniors should be more strongly justified.

INSTRUCTIONS 
This form will be executed in full upon the acceptance of a commission or appointment 
in the Army of the United States. Typewriter will be used, if available. 
FOR THE EXECUTION OF THE OATH OF OFFICE 
1. Whenever any person is elected or appointed to an office of honor or trust under 
the Government of the United States, he is required, before entering upon the duties of hi~ 
office, to take and subscribe the oath prescribed by section 1757, Revised Stat1_1tes. 
2. Oaths of office of Army officers, nurses, and warrant officers should be ta.ken before 
some civil officer who is authorized by the laws of the United States or by the local munici­
pal law to administer oaths; or before a judge advocate or acting judge advocate, the 
president of a general or special court martial, a summary court martial, the judge advocate, 
or assistant judge advocate of a general or special court martial, or the adjutant of a 
command. 
Officers of the Army other than those specified are not authorized by law to 
administer such oaths. 
3. Confirmation by the Senate does not of itself continue in force a recess appointment 
and commission. A new and distinct appointment after confirmation is necessary and a 
new oath of office must be executed for such appointment. 
4. Immediately on receipt of a commission or notification of appointment the 
appointee will, in case of acceptance of the commission or appointment, return to The 
Adjutant General the oath of office on this form properly filled in, subscribed, and attested. 
In case of nonacceptance, the commission or notification of appointment will be returned 
to The Adjutant General by a letter indicating the fact of such nonacceptance.

SRG/al/1650

1. The President, on this date, promoted you, temporarily,
a major in the Army of the United States, under the
provisions of Section 127a, National Defense Act, as amended, with

rank from Februsry 1, 191.2.

2, It is requested that you execute the required oath of
office on the form provided herewith, W.D., A.G,O. Form 0557-T,
and return it without delay directly to this office.

3. Commissions issued to commissioned personnel as a
result of temporary promotion in the Army of the United States
have been discontinued for the period of the War.

FOR THE EXECUTION OF THE OATH OF OFFICE 
1. Whenever any person is elected or appointed to an office of honor or trust under the Government of the United States, he is 
required, before entering upon the duties of his office, to take and subscribe the oath prescribed by section 1757, Revised Statutes. 
2. Oaths of office of Army officers, nurses, and warrant officers should be ta.ken before some civil officer who is authorized by the 
laws of the United States or by the local municipal law to administer oaths; or before a judge advocate or acting judge advocate 
the president of a. general or special cot1rt martial, a. summary court martial, the judge advocate, or assistant judge advocate of ~ 
general or special court martial, or the adjutant of a. command. Officers of the Army other than those specified a.re not· authorized 
by law to administer such oaths, 
a. Confirmation by the Senate does not of itself continue in force a recess appointment and com.mission. 
A new and distinct 
appointment after confirmation is necessary and a new oath of office must be executed for such appointment. 
4. Immediately on receipt of a commission or notification of appointment the appointee will, in case of acceptance of the com­
mission or appointment, return to The Adjutant General the oath of office on thls form properly filled in, subscribed, and attested. 
In case of nonacceptance, the commission or notification of ~ppoin 
eJtt wi~ be returned to The Adjutant General by a letter indi­
cating the fact of such nonacceptance,

FOR THE EXECUTION OF THE OATH OF OFFICE 
1. Whenever any person is elected or appointed to an office of honor or trust under the Government of the United States, he is 
required, before entering upon the duties of his office, to take and subscribe the oath prescribed by section 1757, Revised Statutes. 
2. Oaths of office of Army officers, nurses, and warrant officers should be taken before some civil officer who is authorized by the 
laws of the United States or by the local municipal law to administer oaths; or before a judge advocate or acting judge advocate 
the president of a general or special court martial, a summary court martial, the judge advocate, or assistant judge advocate of ~ 
genera.I or special court martial, or the adjutant of a command. Officers of the Army other than those specified a.re not authorized 
by law to administer such oaths. 
3. Confirmation by the Senate does not of itself continue in force a. recess appointment and commission. A new and distinct 
appointment after confirmation is necessary and a. new oath of office must be executed for such appointment. 
4. Immediately on receipt of a commission or notification of appointment the appointee will, in case of acceptance of the com­
mission or appointment, return to The Adjutant General the oath of office on this form properly filled in, subscribed, and attested. 
In case of nonacceptance, the commission or notification of appointment will be returned to The Adjutant General by a letter indi­
cating the fact of such nonacceptance.

COPY CALIFORNIA “INSTITUTE Of TECHNOLOGY
Pasadena

Daniel Guggenheim feronautical Laboratory

December 14, 1940

Subject: Course of Instruction in Acronautical Engineering at the
California Institute of Technolisy.

To: The Adjutant General (Through Channels).

1. The undersigned desires to explain in detail the course
of instruction he is undertaking at the California Institute of Technology.
It is requested thst this letter be attached to his personnel file,

2. According to the provisions of Par. 44, Special Orders
No. 131, W. D. Washington, 4 June, 1940, the undersigned started the
summer course in aeronautical engineering July 15, 1940. He had previously
in 1935 received the Master of Science degree in Aeronautical Engineering
at this school. Upon entrance this past July he applied for and was
admitted to Candidacy for Uoctor of Philosophy Degree. Because the
regular summer course was too limited for credits toward this latter
degree; additional credits to total sixty-five (65) were taken and received
for the summer term.

Upon detail here from the Materiel Division, “right Field, Ohio,
he had been directed by Bridadier General (Then Lieut. Col.) 0. P. Echols to
further perfect his knowledge of aircraft propellers, on which subject
he has specialized the past six years. As there is no regular course in
propellers, and as he has taken the majority of the aeronautical subjects
during the year 1934-35 in addition to those subjects studied at the Air
Corps Engineering School in 1935-36, it was decided with members of the
faculty that the following schedule of subjects be undertaken. This
schedule meets the requirements for credits for Candidacy for Ph. Degree
and permit specialized study on propeller matters. It is noted thet
although admitted to Candidacy that requirements for Ph. Degree can only
partially be met this school year. Hence no degree will be received,

The schedule follows:-

aS tical Engineering Seminar.
: a Een Be ‘4 8 Research, (Scheduled course totaling
3. Aeronautical Mathematics Reading. ) 45 units.)
4. Technical German. (Regular attendance at this class. It is

required that a test be passed for Ph. Degree
but no credits are allowed Graduate Students
for class attendance.)

The research problem item No. 2 is "The Control and Syn-
chronization of Aircraft Propellers",

The Math Reading item No. 3 consists of regular attendance
at four classes, auditing each for whatever pertinent information
concerning propellers in particular and associated airplane problems
that they may contain.

Advanced Problems in Airplene Design.
Rlesticity Applied to Aeroneutics.

Theoretical Aerodynamics. 7

Engineering Mathematical Physics (Vibrations).

In addition there is assigned and selective reading on vibrations,
acoustics and advanced mathematics.

The above course entails a minimum of sixty-five hours of
study weekly. forty-five units of credits, however, are all that appear
on the records forwarded to the Adjutant Generel. It was the desire
to explain this that prompted this letter.

3. It is requested that if the above schedule, which was
undertaken this first of three school terms, does not meet the approval
of the office Chief of the Air Corps, or The Adjutant General, that the
undersigned be notified prior to January 6, 1941, to enable him to
undertake some different schedule during the last two terms.

s/ H. M. McCoy

H. M. McCoy
Capt., Air Corps

Wer Department, Air Corps, Materiel Division, Washington, D. C., January 23, 1941.
Tos Chief, Air Corps, Washington, D. C,

1. It is recommended that Captain McCoy's schedule of study as given in
basic letter be approved, It is further recommended that he prepare, from original
research, a thesis on the subject, "The Control and Synchronization of Aircraft
Propellers,“ in meeting the partial requirements for the degree of Doctor of
Philosophy.

2. It is recommended that upon completion of this course, Captain McCoy
be detailed to the Materiel Division, Wright Field, for duty in experimental
research and development work on dual rotation propellers, propeller synchronizsers,
and hollow steel blades for propellers of large diameter. le is considered to be
an authority on aircraft propellers. The Materiel Division is badly in need of
officers with this specialty and no suitable reserve officers have been found,
to date.

For the Chief, Materiel Divisions

D. F. PRITCH,
Major, Air Corps,
Administrative Executive.

and Ind. (1-6)

War Dept., Office, Ohief of the Air Corps, Washington, D. C., January 27, 1941 ~

1. It is recommended that the basic communication and paragraph 1
of preceding indorsement be approved.

2. Request for station assignment for thie officer upon completion
of the course will be submitted at a later date.

INSTRUCTIONS 
Thia form will be executed in full upon the acceptance of a commission in the Regular Army on promotion or transfer. It will not be 
used in the case of initial appointment in the Regular Army, Form No. 337- A, A. G. 0., being used for that purpose. Typewriter will be 
used, if available. 
FOR THE EXECUTION OF THE OATH OF OFFICE. 
1. Whenever any person is elected or appointed to an office of honorlor trust under the Government of the United States, he is required, 
before entering upon the duties of his office, to take and subscribe the oath prescribed by section 1757, Revised Statutes. 
2. Oaths of office of Army officers should be taken before some civil officer who is authorized by the laws of the United States or by the local 
municipal law to administer oaths; or before a judge advocate or acting judge advocate, the president of a general or special court-martial, 
a SUilllllllry court-martial, the judge advocate or assistant judge advocate of a general or special court-martial, or the adjutant of a command. 
Officers of the Army other than those specified are not authorized by law to administer such oaths. 
3. Confirmation by the Senate does not of itself continue in force a recess appointment and commission. A new and distinct appoint­
ment after confirmation is necessary and a new oath of office must be executed for such appointment. 
4. Immediately on receipt of a. commission or notification of appointment the appointee will, in case of acceptance of the commission or 
appointment, return to The Adjutant General of the Army the oath of office on this form properly filled in, subscribed, and attested. In case 
of nonacceptance, the commission or notification of appointment will be returned to The Adjutant General of the Army by a letter indicating 
the fact of such nonacceptance.

PERSONNEL ORDERS) ‘
NO. 242 )

EXTRACT

2. Pursuant to Army Regulations 35-1480, the detail to duty involving flying,
effective on the date given below, of each of the following named officers, commis-
sioned in or detailed to the Air Corps, who is fit for duty involving flying, and
who is detailed to duty requiring regular and frequent participation in aerial
flights is hereby announced. ye

The detail to duty involving flying constitutes participation in one or more
of the following: — Routine test flights and test flights of new or overhauled air-
craft or their power plants, instruments, equipment or accessories; prescribed
training of student airplane pilots, student observers or members of aircraft crews;
inspection of the adequacy of flight training material; the efficiency of instruct-
ing personnel; familiarizing pilots or other flying personnel with the operation of
types of aircraft, power plants, instruments, or equipment with which they are inex-
perienced; experimental development of aircraft or parts of aircraft or for experi-
mental development of aviation instruments, equipment or accessories; training for
aircraft gunnery and bombing exercises; administrative or inspection purposes in
connection with air work or for expediting the movements of personnel, material or
mail; training of reserve flying personnel; flights duly authorized for the purpose
of cooperation with other Governmental Departments; ferrying aircraft, aerial scou ing,
reconnaissance, convoy, patrol flights; aerial photography, mapping, ee ERIC EES ;
or training for the performance of any of these duties. hats , ¥

D. CONSIDER CAREFULLY THESE DEFINITIONS, KEEP THEM IN MIND WHEN RATING, TAKING INTO CONSID-

ee pita 2 HIS PERFORMANCE OF DUTY, PERSONAL CHARACTERISTICS, OR PROFESSIONAL QUALI-

UNSATISFACTORY: Performance of the particular duty reported upon or personal characteristics or professional quali-
fications below minimum standard—inefiicient.

SATISFACTORY: Performance of the particular duty reported upon or personal characteristics or professional qualifica-
tions uP to minimum standard—passably efficient.

VERY SATISFACTORY: Performance of the particular duty reported upon in an efficient manner. Personal character-
istics, professional qualifications, or efficiency above that acceptable as SATISFACTORY.

EXCELLENT: Performance of the particular duty reported upon in a very efficient manner. Personal characteristics,
professional qualifications, or efficiency above VERY SATISFACTORY but below SUPERIOR.

SUPERIOR: Outstanding and exceptional performance of the particular duty reported upon. Personal characteristics,
professional qualifications, or efficiency above that considered EXCELLENT.

UNKNOWN: To be used in all cases in which the reporting officer has had insufficient opportunity during the period
covered by this report to observe the officer reported upon to permit a rating as to the performance of the particular
duty, his personal characteristics, or professional qualifications.

istics, professional qualifications, or efficiency above that acceptable as SATISFACTORY.

rofessional qualifications, or efficiency above VERY SATISFACTORY but below SUPERIOR.

INFERIOR: EXCEPTIONALLY POOR performance of duty; EXCEPTIONALLY LACKING in qualification considered.
UNSATISFACTORY: Duty NOT performed AS WELL AS SHOULD REASONABLY BE EXPECTED under circumstances;
not ENTIRELY satisfactory. j
SATISFACTORY: EFFICIENT; duty WELL performed; UP TO STANDARD; qualification SATISFACTORY.
LAR CT) Br performed in a HIGHLY EFFICIENT manner; qualification BETTER THAN usually accepted as
SUPERIOR: PREEMINENTLY EFFICIENT performance of duty; qualified to a PREEMINENT degree.

IV. COMMENTS OF REPORTING OFFICER (Add any comments which ,viii increase the objectivity of the rating. Include strengths or weaknesses not 
covered elso,vhero in thit: report. Bo factual and tJpecHic. lncludo h oro,·n suggostod assignments or type of duty for ,vhich tl1e officer is esp ecially 
qualified. Whcnovor tho officer is rated unsatisfactory or out5tandinll in Section V below, one or mor e actual i ncidents illustrating typical per­
formances wh1'cl1 justify tho ratin ll muat be i"ncluded. Specific comment will bo made o n promotion fitness when U is boliovod the officer reported 
on is particularly well qua.lilied for promotion a n d ahould bo promoted in advance of his contemporaries.) 
During the period of this report, the Physical Security Equipment Agency,of which 
Colonel McCoy was the Director, was in process of being abolished pursuant to directive 
of The Secretary of Defense. Accordingly,the Agency did not function in accordance 
with its Charter, and no actions were engaged in by the Agency or its Director which 
would provide the basis for any comments concerning Colonel McCoy's technical 
competence. The entire period of this report was devoted to the administrative work 
involved in making an orderly termination of the affairs of the Agency. 
This was 
handled jointly by personnel of the Headquarters Command, USAF; the Directorate, OSIJ 
Colonel McCoy; and the few remaining Agency employees. 
While Colonel McCoy cooperated 
in this undertaking, the routine nature of the function performed by Colonel McCoy and 
Agency personnel, does not permit my rating him f or the period involved as to his 
administrative competence. 
I have no knowledge as to the manner in which this officer, during the period 
of this report, carried out his civic responsibilities and represented the Air Force 
in his dealings with the publico

II. DUTY ASSIGNMENT (Givo tho position titlo and AFSC o f duty aui/lnmont durin/1 p oriod concornod and doscribo spocifio dutios. lncludo any ad­
ditional duties.) 
Director, Physical Security Equipment Agency--2016 
Responsible for the administration and supervision of the activities of the Physical 
Security Equipment Agency, a joint Agency operating under terms of a charter from the 
Secretary of Defense, in.the solution of technical physical security and investigative 
problems, including the development of prototype equipment, procurement of service 
test quantities of sue~ equipment, and preparation of training and technical manuals 
pertaining to physical security. Coordinates the efforts of the Agency with those 
activities within and outside the Department of Defense having a material interest in 
and/or responsibility with respect to the Agency programs.

IV. COMMENTS OF REPORTING OFFICER (Add any co,:nmonto which wi1l jncronsa tho objoctlvity of the ratinA. Jnol uda stronAths or weak.nessoo not

formnnces which justify the rat"inA must be included. Speozlio comment w 11l be made on promotion fitness when it is believed the olJioer re/JOrtod 
on is particularly well qualified for promotion and should be promoted in advance of his contemporaries.) 
The Physical Security Equipment Agency, of which Colonel McCoy is the Director, !e 
a joint agency created by Department of Defense Directive and for which the Air Force 
during this particular period was designated Executive Agency. During the entire 
period of this report, the Agency has been in an exceedingly uncertain status, since 
the question as to its continued existence was pending before The Secretary of Defense 
with concrete indications that it might be abolished from one day to the next. 
(A 
Secretary of Defense Directive abolishing the Agency was finally issued on 4 March 1954• 
In view of the fact that the abolition or some other change in the status of the 
Agency was anticipated at al l times, the Agency during the period of this report has 
not act ually functioned to the extent provided for in its charter. The Agency operatioI
therefore, was l imited and Colonel McCoy as its Director was severely handicapped by 
lack of a clear-cut mission and delineation of responsibility, by l ack of the full 
pol icy and procedural guidance which woul d have been provided him by t he joint Policy 
Board had the status of the Agency been more clearly established, and by consequent 
inability to pl an or program Agency efforts toward the attainment of clear-cut 
objectives. During t his period I have had occasional personal and t el ephone contact 
with t his officer as have members of my staff on a more frequent basi s. However, in 
view of the "st and-by" status of the Agency and in fairness t o the officer concerned, 
I do not feel that the nature and extent of my knowledge concerning this officer during 
the specific rating per iod involved is sufficiently concrete t o warrant my advancing 
in an effectiveness rating my opinions or conclusions as to his j ob performance.

This officer has a tremendous capacity t o apply himself to his job. working long 
hours with determination and singleness of purpose. He strives for perfection and is 
impatient with extraneous matters which delay accomplishment of the primary projeot. 
During the period covered by this report, when he has been primarily concerned with 
building his organization and giving it an operating capability, these traits have 
enabled him to accomplish a gre~t deal technically with a very limited staff and under 
difficult conditions. The same intensity of interest and effort has ma.de it difficult 
for him to work out some of the administrative and procedural headaches which have 
been occasioned by the unusual working r elationships of this new organization. He 
i s extremely competent in technical fields and has unusual grasp of management factors.

LAST NAME . FIRST NAME - MIDDLE INITIAL GRADE SERVICE NUMBER
MC COY, HOWARD M. COLONEL 550 A
IV. OVERALL EVALUATION

INSTRUCTIONS: Compare this officer with all officers of the same grade. Remember that extremes of ability are
rare, Be objective and impersonal. Think of the officer's performance over the entire rating period. Place an
(x) in the appropriate box. Be certain to read instructions in Section V before accomplishing your evaluation.

UNSATIS.- AN ACCEPTABLE OFFI- A DEPENDABLE AND TYPICALLY A VERY FINE OFFICER OF GREAT ONE OF THE VERY FEW OUTSTANDING
FACTORY CER EFFECTIVE OFFICER VALUE TO THE SERVJCE OFFICERS 1 KNOW

V¥. COMMENTS OF REPORTING OFFICER (Include any strengths or weaknesses not covered elsewhere in this report. Whenever
the officer is rated unsatisfactory or outstanding one or more actual incidents illustrating typical performances which
justify the rating must be included. Specific comment will be made on promotion fitness when it is believed the officer
reported on is outstandingly well qualified for promotion and should be promoted in advance of his contemporaries. Include
herein suggested assignments or type of duty for which the officer is especially qualified. A definite statement will
be made as to the degree to which this officer exercises judgment in economical management of personnel and resources
under his supervision, commensurate with his responsibilities.)

My evaluation of Colonel McCoy is based on infrequent contacts and routine
observance of the results of his work. One outstanding feature is his enthusiasm,
interest and drive in accomplishing his present assignments I believe him to be
highly competent in his presently assigned area and the results of his work indicate
accomplishment of his assignment in an outstanding fashion. He undoubtedly possesses
imagination, initiative and ingenuity. Minor administrative incidents which have
occurred during his assignment indicate that he may be somewhat reluctant to accept
regulations and routine administrative controls when these appear to interfere with
the effective accomplishment of his mission. In this respect, his judgment may be
subject to question, however, as of this date, with the information available to me,
I would rate him as a superior officer of extreme value to the service, This
officer is not an empire builder and exercises sound judgment in the economical
management of personnel and resources under his supervisione

MEMORANDUM FOR: 
The Air Adjutant General 
Department of the Air Force 
Washington 25, D. c. 
SUBJECT:. 
Efficiency Report in the case of Colonel Howard M. 
McCoy, covering the period JO August 1950 to 
13 March 1951 
1. 
Subject officer has exhibited a thorough lmowledge and 
understanding of intelligence during his assignment in this office. 
While he was not assigned to a position requiring the handling of 
administrative details or supervising personnel, he demonstrated 
an ability for getting along with others, encouraging ideas and 
assisting my subordinates in their work. 
He is capable of develop­
ing ideas with initiative and imagination. 
2. 
The military appearance and attitude of this officer is 
in keeping with the high standards of the United States Air Force.

INSTRUCTIONS
(SEE AR 600-185)

4. Efficiency ratings comprise an evaluation of an officer in comparision with others of the same grade. Ina large group of officers, such as are normally assigned to an Infantry division,
the lew of averages will establish that about twenty percent of the officers of each grade may be superior, about twenty percent may be in the two lower classifications of very satisfactory and
satisfactory (Unsatisfactory officers should be identified promptly and reclassified), the remaining sixty per cent who constitute the mass of thoroushly capable officers would fall into the excellent
category. Commanders of regiments and large units must analyze the reports prepared within their organization and whenever the distribution varies markedly from the ebove the reasons must
be sousht. It is-highly important that officers who are genuinely superior be identified by their ratings so that their talents may be fully utilized. It is also important to identify those officers
whose qualifications and standards of performance of duty are above the minimum required, but who are clearly below the standard established by the mass of officers of their grade. A series
of reports of an individual officer should present a highly eccurate evaluation of his worth.

2. Priogto filling in Section G of this report consider carefully the following definitions and the numerical values assigned to each. Keep them in mind when rating, taking into considera-
tion the lensth of service of officer you are rating and the opportunities afforded him, which might have a bearing upon his performance of duty, personal characteristics, or professional qualifice-
tions.

Unsatisfactory: Performance of the particular duty reported upon or personal characteristics or professional qualifications below minimum standards—inefficient.
Satisfactory: Performance of the particular duty reported upon or personal characteristics or professional qualifications up to minimum standard—passably efficient.

Very Satisfactory: Performance of the particular duty reported upon in an efficient manner. Personal characteristics, professional qualifications, or efficiency above that acceptable as sat-
factory.

Excellent: Performance of the particular duty reported upon in a very efficient manner. Personal characteristics, professional qualifications, or efficiency above very satisfactory but below
superior.

_ Superior: Outstanding and exceptional performance of the particular duty reported upon. Personal SCRCUIES professional qualifications, or efficiency ayers that considered excellent.

Unknown: To be used in all cases in which the re officer has had insufficient opportunity during the peicd cov ghis report to observe the officer reported upon to permit
a rating as to the performance of the particular duty, nal characteristics, or professional qualifications.

4. Efficiency ratings comprise an evaluation of an officer In comparision with others of the same grade. In a large group of officers, such‘es ere normally assigned to an infantry division,
the law of averages will establish that about twenty percent of the officers of each grade may be superior, about twenty percent may be in the two lower classifications of very satisfactory and
satisfactory (Unsatisfactory officers should be identified promptly and reclassified); the remaining sixty per cent who constitute the mass of thoroughly capable officers would fall into the excellent
category. Commanders of regiments and large units must-analyze the reports prepered within their organization and whenever the distribution varies markedly from the above the reasons must
besousht. It is highly important that officers who are genuinely superior be identified by their ratings so that their talents. may be fully utilized, It is also important to identify those officers
whose qualifications and standards of performance of duty ere above the minimum required, but who are clearly below the standard established by the mass of officers of their grade. A series

INSTRUCTIONS
(SEE AR 600-185)

1. Efficiency ratings comprise an evaluation of an officer in comparision With others of the same grade. Ina large group of officers, such as are normally assigned to an infantry division,
the law of averages will establish that about twenty percent of the officers of each grade may be superior; about twenty percent may be in the two lower classifications of very satisfactory and
satisfactory (Unsatisfactory officers should be identified promptly and reclassified); the remaining sixty per cent who constitute the mass of thoroughly capable officers would fall into the excellent
category. Commanders of regiments and large units must analyze the reports prepared within their organization and whenever the distribution varies markedly from the above the reasons must
be sought. It is highly important that officers who are genuinely superior be identified by their ratings so that their talents may be fully utilized. It is also important to identify those officers .
whose qualificetions and standards of performance of duty are above the minimum required, but who are clearly below the standard established by the mass of officers of their grade. A series
of reports of an individual officer should present a highly accurate evaluation of his worth.

2. Prior to filling in Section G of this report consider carefully the following definitions and the numerical values assigned to each. Keep them in mind when rating, taking into considera-
tion the length of service of officer you are rating and the opportunities afforded him, which might have a bearing upon his performance of duty, personal characteristics, or professional qualifica-
tions.

Unsatisfactory: Performance of the particular duty reported upon or personal characteristics or professional qualifications below minimum standards—inefficient.
Satisfactory: Performance of the particular duty reported upon or personal characteristics or professional qualifications up to minimum standard—passably efficient.

Very Satisfactory: Performance of the particuler duty reported upon in an efficient manner. Personal characteristics, professional qualifications, or efficiency above that acceptable as sat-
factory. |

Excellent: Performance of the particular duty reported upon in a very efficient manner. Personal characteristics, professional qualifications, or efficiency above very satisfactory but below
superior.

.
Superior: Outstanding and exceptional performance of the particular duty reported upon. Personal characteristics, professional qualifications, or efficiency above that considered excellent.

Unknown: To be used in all cases in which the jamorting officer has hed insufficient opportunity during the period coy; 1 by this report to observe the officer reported upon to permit
a rating 6s to the performance of the particular duty, ‘onal characteristics, or professional qualifications. ’

BEINGS EON HIS PERFORMANCE OF DUTY, PERSONAL CHARACTERISTICS, OR PROFESSIONAL QUALI.
FI I

UNSATISFACTORY: Performance of the particular duty reported upon or personal characteristics or professional quali-

- fications below minimum standard—inefficient. -

SATISFACTORY: Performance of the particular duty reported upon or personal characteristics or professional qualifica-
tions up to minimum standard—passably efficient.

VERY SATISFACTORY: Performance of the particular duty reported upon in an efficient manner. Personal character-
istics, professional qualifications, or efficiency above that acceptable as SATISFACTORY. fp

EXCELLENT: Performance of the particular duty reported upon in a very efficient manner. Personal characteristics,
professional qualifications, or efficiency above VERY SATISFACTORY but below SUPERIOR.

SUPERIOR: Outstanding and exceptional performance of the particular duty reported upon. Personal characteristics,
professional qualifications, or efficiency above that considered EXCELLENT.

UNKNOWN: To be used in all cases in which the reporting officer has had insufficient opportunity during the period
covered by this report to observe the officer reported upon to permit a rating as to the performance ‘of the particular
duty, his personal characteristics, or professional qualifications. 3

SEE ON EON HIS PERFORMANCE OF DUTY, PERSONAL CHARACTERISTICS, OR PROFESSIONAL QUALI-

UNSATISFACTORY: Performance of the particular duty reported upon or personal characteristics or professional quali-
fications below minimum standard—inefficient.

SATISFACTORY: Performance of the particular duty reported upon or personal characteristics or professional qualifica-
tions up to minimum standard—passably efficient.

VERY SATISFACTORY: Performance of the particular duty reported upon in an efficient manner. Personal character-
istics, professional qualifications, or efficiency above that acceptable as SATISFACTORY.

EXCELLENT: Performance of the particular duty reported upon in a very efficient manner. Personal characteristics,
professional qualifications, or efficiency above VERY SATISFACTORY but below SUPERIOR.

SUPERIOR: Outstanding and exceptional performance of the particular duty reported upon. Personal characteristics,
professional qualifications, or efficiency above that considered EXCELLENT. E:

UNKNOWN: To be used in all cases in which the reporting officer has had insufficient opportunity during the period
covered by this report to observe the officer reported upon to permit a rating as to the-performance of the particular
duty, his personal characteristics, or professional qualifications.

E. DUTIES HE PERFORMED: (State separately. Where possible show duration of each in months. Example: Co. Comdr.
ordinary garrison training, 8 mos. Summary court, 6 mos. Brig. Adj. prepared training schedules, Supply Officer.) In
describing the manner of performance of duty, use one of six classifications as given under D, and consider carefully the
obstacles encountered by the individual in the performance of each duty listed. THE OPINIONS EXPRESSED UNDER
“MANNER OF PERFORMANCE” ARE BASED ON—
